It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.

It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.

These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I clean buildings, you know, and some of those old places have trash rooms and basements and all that, and I keep thinking I’m going to see a rat. Even if I know, like, it’s just an old building and probably nothing’s going to happen, I get so worked up I can’t do parts of my job anymore.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… it comes on really fast. Like, if I’m in a basement and I hear scratching by a wall, or I see something move near a pipe, my body just reacts before I can even think. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I just feel like I have to get out right then.\n\nAnd it’s not even always a rat, you know? These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same fear reaction to rats if you encountered one in a different context, such as at home or in a park?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Like right away my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get away before I can even think straight. It’s not like I calmly check what it is. I just react.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Maybe a little, I guess, because those old buildings and trash rooms are where I expect them. Like the minute I see a basement door or those big bins, my head goes there.

But honestly, it’s gotten so bad that even just thinking I might see a rat makes me back out of parts of my cleaning job. And I know, logically, it’s probably just an old building, or garbage smells, or pipes making noise—not like it’s some huge danger every time. My aunt used to live in this old apartment where the walls made noises all night, and she’d say, “That’s just the building settling,” but for me now, I hear something like that and I’m already thinking rat.

So… yes, maybe it’d be less if I weren’t around those places so much. But the fear is still there, and it’s strong enough that I can’t do parts of the work anymore.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n10\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because it’s gotten really hard for me at work. I clean buildings, you know, and some of those old places have trash rooms and basements and all that, and I keep thinking I’m going to see a rat. Even if I know, like, it’s just an old building and probably nothing’s going to happen, I get so worked up I can’t do parts of my job anymore.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… it comes on really fast. Like, if I’m in a basement and I hear scratching by a wall, or I see something move near a pipe, my body just reacts before I can even think. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I just feel like I have to get out right then.\n\nAnd it’s not even always a rat, you know? These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Like right away my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get away before I can even think straight. It’s not like I calmly check what it is. I just react.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would be less intense if you weren't working in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, because those old buildings and trash rooms are where I expect them. Like the minute I see a basement door or those big bins, my head goes there.\n\nBut honestly, it’s gotten so bad that even just thinking I might see a rat makes me back out of parts of my cleaning job. And I know, logically, it’s probably just an old building, or garbage smells, or pipes making noise—not like it’s some huge danger every time. My aunt used to live in this old apartment where the walls made noises all night, and she’d say, “That’s just the building settling,” but for me now, I hear something like that and I’m already thinking rat.\n\nSo… yes, maybe it’d be less if I weren’t around those places so much. But the fear is still there, and it’s strong enough that I can’t do parts of the work anymore.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Completely free? I don’t think so. I mean, maybe it would help not being around those old basements and trash rooms all day—because that’s where my mind really starts going. But even the thought of seeing a rat now has gotten so bad that it’s stopping me from doing parts of my cleaning job.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n11\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because it’s gotten really hard for me at work. I clean buildings, you know, and some of those old places have trash rooms and basements and all that, and I keep thinking I’m going to see a rat. Even if I know, like, it’s just an old building and probably nothing’s going to happen, I get so worked up I can’t do parts of my job anymore.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… it comes on really fast. Like, if I’m in a basement and I hear scratching by a wall, or I see something move near a pipe, my body just reacts before I can even think. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I just feel like I have to get out right then.\n\nAnd it’s not even always a rat, you know? These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same fear reaction to rats if you encountered one in a different context, such as at home or in a park?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Like right away my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get away before I can even think straight. It’s not like I calmly check what it is. I just react.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would be less intense if you weren't working in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, because those old buildings and trash rooms are where I expect them. Like the minute I see a basement door or those big bins, my head goes there.\n\nBut honestly, it’s gotten so bad that even just thinking I might see a rat makes me back out of parts of my cleaning job. And I know, logically, it’s probably just an old building, or garbage smells, or pipes making noise—not like it’s some huge danger every time. My aunt used to live in this old apartment where the walls made noises all night, and she’d say, “That’s just the building settling,” but for me now, I hear something like that and I’m already thinking rat.\n\nSo… yes, maybe it’d be less if I weren’t around those places so much. But the fear is still there, and it’s strong enough that I can’t do parts of the work anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be completely free of this rat-related fear if you were to change jobs and no longer work in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Completely free? I don’t think so. I mean, maybe it would help not being around those old basements and trash rooms all day—because that’s where my mind really starts going. But even the thought of seeing a rat now has gotten so bad that it’s stopping me from doing parts of my cleaning job.\n\nAnd I know, I do know, it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in some real danger every time. My husband says, “It’s just a job, don’t let it get in your head,” but it does. So changing jobs might make it easier, but I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. No, I don’t think it would completely go away. Maybe I wouldn’t run into it as much if I wasn’t working, and I wouldn’t have to deal with those trash rooms and old buildings—those places make it worse for me.

My sister always says I overthink everything—she says that about a lot of things, actually, like when I check the stove twice—but with this it really stops me. I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. And I know it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not really dangerous, but my fear doesn’t listen to that.

From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n12\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because it’s gotten really hard for me at work. I clean buildings, you know, and some of those old places have trash rooms and basements and all that, and I keep thinking I’m going to see a rat. Even if I know, like, it’s just an old building and probably nothing’s going to happen, I get so worked up I can’t do parts of my job anymore.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… it comes on really fast. Like, if I’m in a basement and I hear scratching by a wall, or I see something move near a pipe, my body just reacts before I can even think. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I just feel like I have to get out right then.\n\nAnd it’s not even always a rat, you know? These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same fear reaction to rats if you encountered one in a different context, such as at home or in a park?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Maybe I wouldn’t run into it as much if I wasn’t working, and I wouldn’t have to deal with those trash rooms and old buildings—those places make it worse for me.\n\nBut I’m here because even just the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same fear reaction to rats if you encountered one in a different context, such as at home or in a park?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Like right away my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get away before I can even think straight. It’s not like I calmly check what it is. I just react.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would be less intense if you weren't working in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, because those old buildings and trash rooms are where I expect them. Like the minute I see a basement door or those big bins, my head goes there.\n\nBut honestly, it’s gotten so bad that even just thinking I might see a rat makes me back out of parts of my cleaning job. And I know, logically, it’s probably just an old building, or garbage smells, or pipes making noise—not like it’s some huge danger every time. My aunt used to live in this old apartment where the walls made noises all night, and she’d say, “That’s just the building settling,” but for me now, I hear something like that and I’m already thinking rat.\n\nSo… yes, maybe it’d be less if I weren’t around those places so much. But the fear is still there, and it’s strong enough that I can’t do parts of the work anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be completely free of this rat-related fear if you were to change jobs and no longer work in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Completely free? I don’t think so. I mean, maybe it would help not being around those old basements and trash rooms all day—because that’s where my mind really starts going. But even the thought of seeing a rat now has gotten so bad that it’s stopping me from doing parts of my cleaning job.\n\nAnd I know, I do know, it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in some real danger every time. My husband says, “It’s just a job, don’t let it get in your head,” but it does. So changing jobs might make it easier, but I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would completely go away if you were no longer working?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it would completely go away. Maybe I wouldn’t run into it as much if I wasn’t working, and I wouldn’t have to deal with those trash rooms and old buildings—those places make it worse for me.\n\nBut I’m here because even just the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad. Like, I’ll look at a room or a corner and start thinking about it before anything even happens. My sister always says I overthink everything—she says that about a lot of things, actually, like when I check the stove twice—but with this it really stops me. I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. Maybe… a little less if it wasn’t at work, I guess? Like if I saw one outside by an alley or something, I might be able to walk away faster and not have it be tied to all the rooms I’m supposed to clean.

I clean buildings, you know, and some of those old places have trash rooms and basements and all that, and I keep thinking I’m going to see a rat. Even if I know, like, it’s just an old building and probably nothing’s going to happen, I get so worked up I can’t do parts of my job anymore.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… it comes on really fast. Like, if I’m in a basement and I hear scratching by a wall, or I see something move near a pipe, my body just reacts before I can even think. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I just feel like I have to get out right then.\n\nAnd it’s not even always a rat, you know? These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same fear reaction to rats if you encountered one in a different context, such as at home or in a park?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Like right away my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get away before I can even think straight. It’s not like I calmly check what it is. I just react.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would be less intense if you weren't working in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, because those old buildings and trash rooms are where I expect them. Like the minute I see a basement door or those big bins, my head goes there.\n\nBut honestly, it’s gotten so bad that even just thinking I might see a rat makes me back out of parts of my cleaning job. And I know, logically, it’s probably just an old building, or garbage smells, or pipes making noise—not like it’s some huge danger every time. My aunt used to live in this old apartment where the walls made noises all night, and she’d say, “That’s just the building settling,” but for me now, I hear something like that and I’m already thinking rat.\n\nSo… yes, maybe it’d be less if I weren’t around those places so much. But the fear is still there, and it’s strong enough that I can’t do parts of the work anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be completely free of this rat-related fear if you were to change jobs and no longer work in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Completely free? I don’t think so. I mean, maybe it would help not being around those old basements and trash rooms all day—because that’s where my mind really starts going. But even the thought of seeing a rat now has gotten so bad that it’s stopping me from doing parts of my cleaning job.\n\nAnd I know, I do know, it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in some real danger every time. My husband says, “It’s just a job, don’t let it get in your head,” but it does. So changing jobs might make it easier, but I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would completely go away if you were no longer working?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it would completely go away. Maybe I wouldn’t run into it as much if I wasn’t working, and I wouldn’t have to deal with those trash rooms and old buildings—those places make it worse for me.\n\nBut I’m here because even just the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad. Like, I’ll look at a room or a corner and start thinking about it before anything even happens. My sister always says I overthink everything—she says that about a lot of things, actually, like when I check the stove twice—but with this it really stops me. I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. And I know it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not really dangerous, but my fear doesn’t listen to that.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be less intense if you were to encounter one in a context unrelated to your work?\n\nPatient: Maybe… a little less if it wasn’t at work, I guess? Like if I saw one outside by an alley or something, I might be able to walk away faster and not have it be tied to all the rooms I’m supposed to clean.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know that it would be that much less. The thought of seeing a rat at all has gotten so bad that I freeze up before anything even happens. It’s like with the trash rooms and old buildings, I can tell myself, “Okay, it’s probably just the building, it’s not really dangerous,” but my body doesn’t listen. And then I can’t do parts of my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going around it. I think work makes it worse, but the fear is really about the rat, not just the job.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Never bundle several symptoms, or several attributes of one symptom, into a single question — e.g. asking whether a symptom is present and how long it has lasted at the same time.\n- If you need to ask about more than one thing, ask the one that tells you the most now and come back to the rest later.\n- Keep the question clear enough for the patient to understand.\n- Respond in English.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"intent\": \"<what you want to find out with this question>\", \"question\": \"<how you actually ask it in the interview>\"}\n"

Like, if I’m in a basement and I hear scratching by a wall, or I see something move near a pipe, my body just reacts before I can even think. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I just feel like I have to get out right then.\n\nAnd it’s not even always a rat, you know? These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same fear reaction to rats if you encountered one in a different context, such as at home or in a park?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Like right away my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get away before I can even think straight. It’s not like I calmly check what it is. I just react.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would be less intense if you weren't working in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, because those old buildings and trash rooms are where I expect them. Like the minute I see a basement door or those big bins, my head goes there.\n\nBut honestly, it’s gotten so bad that even just thinking I might see a rat makes me back out of parts of my cleaning job. And I know, logically, it’s probably just an old building, or garbage smells, or pipes making noise—not like it’s some huge danger every time. My aunt used to live in this old apartment where the walls made noises all night, and she’d say, “That’s just the building settling,” but for me now, I hear something like that and I’m already thinking rat.\n\nSo… yes, maybe it’d be less if I weren’t around those places so much. But the fear is still there, and it’s strong enough that I can’t do parts of the work anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be completely free of this rat-related fear if you were to change jobs and no longer work in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Completely free? I don’t think so. I mean, maybe it would help not being around those old basements and trash rooms all day—because that’s where my mind really starts going. But even the thought of seeing a rat now has gotten so bad that it’s stopping me from doing parts of my cleaning job.\n\nAnd I know, I do know, it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in some real danger every time. My husband says, “It’s just a job, don’t let it get in your head,” but it does. So changing jobs might make it easier, but I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would completely go away if you were no longer working?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it would completely go away. Maybe I wouldn’t run into it as much if I wasn’t working, and I wouldn’t have to deal with those trash rooms and old buildings—those places make it worse for me.\n\nBut I’m here because even just the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad. Like, I’ll look at a room or a corner and start thinking about it before anything even happens. My sister always says I overthink everything—she says that about a lot of things, actually, like when I check the stove twice—but with this it really stops me. I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. And I know it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not really dangerous, but my fear doesn’t listen to that.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be less intense if you were to encounter one in a context unrelated to your work?\n\nPatient: Maybe… a little less if it wasn’t at work, I guess? Like if I saw one outside by an alley or something, I might be able to walk away faster and not have it be tied to all the rooms I’m supposed to clean.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know that it would be that much less. The thought of seeing a rat at all has gotten so bad that I freeze up before anything even happens. It’s like with the trash rooms and old buildings, I can tell myself, “Okay, it’s probably just the building, it’s not really dangerous,” but my body doesn’t listen. And then I can’t do parts of my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going around it. I think work makes it worse, but the fear is really about the rat, not just the job.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be the same if you were to encounter one in a situation that had nothing to do with your job?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. Like, if it had nothing to do with work, maybe I could just get away from it and not have to go back into that room. But the fear itself, it’s really the rat. Just the thought of seeing one now gets me worked up.\n\nAt work it’s worse because of the old buildings and trash rooms, and then I start thinking, “There’s going to be one in there,” before I even open the door. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in real danger, but it still gets so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job.\n\nMy husband tells me not to let it run my life, and I know he’s right, but… it doesn’t feel that simple.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be triggered if you were to encounter one in a situation that is completely unrelated to cleaning or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would. Like even if I wasn’t cleaning, if I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, my whole body would just react right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight.\n\nIt’s not only the job, even though work makes it worse. I mean, my husband was fixing something under the sink once and I kept thinking about the pipes and—sorry, that’s not really what you asked. But yes, if it was totally unrelated to cleaning, I’d still get scared if I thought there was a rat there.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"

Like, if it had nothing to do with work, maybe I could just get away from it and not have to go back into that room. But the fear itself, it’s really the rat. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in real danger, but it still gets so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job.\n\nMy husband tells me not to let it run my life, and I know he’s right, but… it doesn’t feel that simple.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be triggered if you were to encounter one in a situation that is completely unrelated to cleaning or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would. Like even if I wasn’t cleaning, if I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, my whole body would just react right away. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get out before I can even think straight.\n\nIt’s not only the job, even though work makes it worse. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I think it would still be really bad, yes. Maybe being at home should make me feel safer, but if I actually thought I was going to see a rat, I don’t think my body would care where I was.

It’s gotten to where just the thought of seeing one is enough to mess me up. And at work, with the old buildings and trash rooms, I can tell myself, “It’s probably just the place, it’s not really dangerous,” but I still can’t make myself do some parts of my cleaning job anymore.

Sorry, I know you asked about home. I guess what I’m saying is, the place might change it a little, but the rat is the part that scares me.

    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n16\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF40.1, F40.2\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hello, welcome to the clinic. What brings you in to see me today?\n\nPatient: Um, yeah… I’m here because it’s gotten really hard for me at work. I clean buildings, you know, and some of those old places have trash rooms and basements and all that, and I keep thinking I’m going to see a rat. Even if I know, like, it’s just an old building and probably nothing’s going to happen, I get so worked up I can’t do parts of my job anymore.\n\nIt sounds silly when I say it, but it’s gotten pretty bad.\n\nDoctor: Do you ever have episodes where you feel an intense fear or discomfort that reaches a peak within minutes?\n\nPatient: Yes… it comes on really fast. Like, if I’m in a basement and I hear scratching by a wall, or I see something move near a pipe, my body just reacts before I can even think. My heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I just feel like I have to get out right then.\n\nAnd it’s not even always a rat, you know? These buildings make all kinds of noises—pipes, vents, old walls settling. My husband says old buildings are just noisy, and he’s probably right, but in that moment it doesn’t matter. I’m already halfway to the door before my brain catches up.\n\nDoctor: Is your fear mainly about seeing a rat, or are you also afraid of other things in those situations?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly the rat. Like, the whole thing is the thought that I’m going to see one, or one’s going to run out from behind something. The trash rooms and basements just… they set it off because that’s where I picture them being.\n\nI mean, old buildings are gross sometimes anyway—there’s smells, pipes, bags leaking, all that—and I’ve cleaned plenty of nasty places. But those things don’t stop me the same way. It’s really the idea of a rat being there that gets so bad I can’t do parts of the job, even when I know it’s probably just the building and not actually dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of other animals as well, or is it mainly rats that cause you this fear?\n\nPatient: It’s really mainly rats. I mean, I don’t love bugs or anything—who does, especially in some of these buildings where people leave food wrappers everywhere—but I can still do my work with that.\n\nWith rats, though, it’s different. Just the thought of seeing one, like in a trash room or an old basement, gets so bad that I can’t go in or I have to stop what I’m doing. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building making noise, not necessarily a rat or something dangerous, but that doesn’t seem to help much.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats happen only when you're at work in those old buildings, or can it also be triggered in other situations or places?\n\nPatient: It’s mostly at work, because that’s where I’m dealing with the trash rooms and the old basements and all those little corners where I start thinking one’s going to come out. I mean, I’ll hear a noise by a pipe and my mind goes straight there.\n\nBut honestly it’s the thought of seeing a rat that’s the problem, not just the building. That’s why I’m here — it’s gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when part of me knows it’s probably just an old building making noise or a trash room being gross, not really something dangerous.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats ever bother you when you're not at work?\n\nPatient: I mean… not really the same way. I’ll think about it sometimes, like if I’m walking past an alley or there’s a dumpster, but it’s work where it really gets me because I have to go into those trash rooms and basements.\n\nThat’s why I finally came in. The thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore, even when I know it’s probably just an old building or a gross trash room and not actually dangerous. It’s frustrating, because I’ve cleaned all kinds of messes for years, you know, and now this one thing just stops me.\n\nDoctor: Does your fear of rats affect your daily life outside of work in any way?\n\nPatient: Outside of work… I guess it’s more that work follows me home, if that makes sense. Like I’ll be at home thinking, “Tomorrow I have that route with the basement,” and then I’m already worrying about whether I’m going to have to refuse it again.\n\nI’ve had to put off or say no to cleaning certain buildings, and my supervisor’s been asking if I can really be counted on for the regular routes. And that’s… that’s scary, because I need the better shifts. I was hoping maybe to get considered for a lead spot someday, you know, since I’ve been doing this a long time. My cousin keeps telling me jobs are hard to find now, and she’s not wrong. So even if I’m not seeing rats outside of work, it’s affecting my life because I’m worried I’m messing up my job.\n\nDoctor: Have you ever had a similar intense fear reaction to anything else, like other animals or specific situations, outside of seeing rats?\n\nPatient: I don’t think so… I mean, not like this. I don’t love bugs or anything, but who does, right? My sister screams if there’s a spider in the bathtub, and I’m usually the one who used to just get a cup and deal with it.\n\nIt’s really the rats that… I don’t know, they just get in my head. Other animals or places don’t make me freeze up the same way. I can’t think of anything else that hits me like that.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would have the same fear reaction to rats if you encountered one in a different context, such as at home or in a park?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think I would. I mean, I don’t really go looking for them in parks or anything, and at home I try to keep everything clean—my mother used to say if you leave one crumb out you’re inviting trouble, which I know is not the point, but still.\n\nIf I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, even if it wasn’t at work, my body would just go. Like right away my heart starts pounding, my hands shake, and I feel like I have to get away before I can even think straight. It’s not like I calmly check what it is. I just react.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would be less intense if you weren't working in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Maybe a little, I guess, because those old buildings and trash rooms are where I expect them. Like the minute I see a basement door or those big bins, my head goes there.\n\nBut honestly, it’s gotten so bad that even just thinking I might see a rat makes me back out of parts of my cleaning job. And I know, logically, it’s probably just an old building, or garbage smells, or pipes making noise—not like it’s some huge danger every time. My aunt used to live in this old apartment where the walls made noises all night, and she’d say, “That’s just the building settling,” but for me now, I hear something like that and I’m already thinking rat.\n\nSo… yes, maybe it’d be less if I weren’t around those places so much. But the fear is still there, and it’s strong enough that I can’t do parts of the work anymore.\n\nDoctor: Do you think you would be completely free of this rat-related fear if you were to change jobs and no longer work in those old buildings and trash rooms?\n\nPatient: Completely free? I don’t think so. I mean, maybe it would help not being around those old basements and trash rooms all day—because that’s where my mind really starts going. But even the thought of seeing a rat now has gotten so bad that it’s stopping me from doing parts of my cleaning job.\n\nAnd I know, I do know, it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in some real danger every time. My husband says, “It’s just a job, don’t let it get in your head,” but it does. So changing jobs might make it easier, but I don’t think it would just disappear.\n\nDoctor: Do you think your fear of rats would completely go away if you were no longer working?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t think it would completely go away. Maybe I wouldn’t run into it as much if I wasn’t working, and I wouldn’t have to deal with those trash rooms and old buildings—those places make it worse for me.\n\nBut I’m here because even just the thought of seeing a rat has gotten so bad. Like, I’ll look at a room or a corner and start thinking about it before anything even happens. My sister always says I overthink everything—she says that about a lot of things, actually, like when I check the stove twice—but with this it really stops me. I can’t do parts of my cleaning job anymore. And I know it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not really dangerous, but my fear doesn’t listen to that.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be less intense if you were to encounter one in a context unrelated to your work?\n\nPatient: Maybe… a little less if it wasn’t at work, I guess? Like if I saw one outside by an alley or something, I might be able to walk away faster and not have it be tied to all the rooms I’m supposed to clean.\n\nBut honestly, I don’t know that it would be that much less. The thought of seeing a rat at all has gotten so bad that I freeze up before anything even happens. It’s like with the trash rooms and old buildings, I can tell myself, “Okay, it’s probably just the building, it’s not really dangerous,” but my body doesn’t listen. And then I can’t do parts of my job.\n\nSorry, I’m kind of going around it. I think work makes it worse, but the fear is really about the rat, not just the job.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be the same if you were to encounter one in a situation that had nothing to do with your job?\n\nPatient: I think… yes and no. Like, if it had nothing to do with work, maybe I could just get away from it and not have to go back into that room. But the fear itself, it’s really the rat. Just the thought of seeing one now gets me worked up.\n\nAt work it’s worse because of the old buildings and trash rooms, and then I start thinking, “There’s going to be one in there,” before I even open the door. And I know in my head it’s probably just an old building or a trash room, not like I’m in real danger, but it still gets so bad that I can’t do parts of my cleaning job.\n\nMy husband tells me not to let it run my life, and I know he’s right, but… it doesn’t feel that simple.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be triggered if you were to encounter one in a situation that is completely unrelated to cleaning or your work?\n\nPatient: Yes, I think it would. Like even if I wasn’t cleaning, if I heard scratching near a wall or saw something move by a pipe, my whole body would just react right away. But yes, if it was totally unrelated to cleaning, I’d still get scared if I thought there was a rat there.\n\nDoctor: Would your fear of rats be just as intense if you were to encounter one in a familiar, safe place like your own home?\n\nPatient: I think it would still be really bad, yes. And at work, with the old buildings and trash rooms, I can tell myself, “It’s probably just the place, it’s not really dangerous,” but I still can’t make myself do some parts of my cleaning job anymore.\n\nSorry, I know you asked about home. I guess what I’m saying is, the place might change it a little, but the rat is the part that scares me.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. If it did not change, leave \"note\" empty.\n- If the candidates have narrowed to one and you are confident enough that no further questioning is needed, return true for is_final.\n\n[Output Format — JSON only, no other text]\n{\"candidates\": [\"<ICD-10 code>\", \"...\"], \"note\": \"<why the list changed, or empty>\", \"is_final\": <true or false>}\n"
